Transcript
Intro

0:01 · This is the largest green roof in New York City.

0:04 · It’s in the middle of bustling Manhattan, on top of the Javits Convention Center— a behemoth of a building with a six-block footprint.

0:13 · But the roof hasn’t always been green.

0:16 · Before 2014, it was totally barren.

0:20 · Now, just a few years later, when people walk out onto the roof, they say things like: “Amazing,” “That’s great,” “I didn’t expect this,” “What a surprise.”

0:28 · But besides the great reactions, there are practical benefits.

0:31 · We have 300,000 bees.

0:34 · We’ve seen 25 different species of birds on the roof.

0:36 · We reduced the temperature up here by about six degrees Fahrenheit.

0:39 · And we also save about 7 million gallons of stormwater runoff that’s absorbed in the soil and the plants that are on our roof.

0:46 · We’ve been able to reduce our energy

consumption by about 26 percent, and that translated for us last year

into a saving of about 3 million dollars.

0:55 · That is impressive.

Green Roofs

0:58 · How does one green roof get so much done?

1:01 · And what are all of the NOT green roofs in New York City doing with their lives?

1:06 · First, let’s see how much roof space actually exists.

1:09 · You know, they are a major part of urban landscapes.

1:12 · The the round number that most of us think exists is about 1 billion square feet.

1:18 · One billion with a ‘b.’

1:20 · That’s like 22 times the size of Central Park, in roofs.

1:24 · And in most cities, what isn’t roof is another impermeable surface like road, sidewalk, or parking

lot.

1:31 · And these surfaces create a lot of problems for cities That green roofs can actually help solve.

stormwater runoff

1:38 · Let’s take a look at three of these issues. The first is

stormwater runoff.

1:42 · Rainwater goes in pipes. It combines

with what’s called sanitary water.

1:48 · When there’s too much rainwater, which happens hundreds of times a year around Manhattan, the wastewater treatment

systems shut down, and valves just let the water just, you know, shunt out into the rivers. You know, it’s got human sewage.

2:01 · It’s the biggest source of pathogens to

the Manhattan Island.

2:04 · With green roofs, that water instead of going into the

drain and polluting the environment, ends up just evaporating the atmosphere

through the leaves of these plants.

heat island effect

2:13 · And then, there’s what scientists call the “heat island effect,” which basically means that cities are significantly warmer than the surrounding areas.

2:20 · I’ve seen asphalt roofs get up easily 170, 180 degrees Fahrenheit in the strong sunlight.

2:26 · They absorb about 90 to 95 percent of the sunlight.

2:30 · Green roofs, by comparison are close to air temperatures.

habitat loss

2:34 · Another problem green roofs can help solve is habitat loss.

2:37 · Cities can shrink or destroy the green space that’s crucial for certain plants and

animals to survive.

2:43 · Currently a lot of species might occur in smaller parks throughout the city that are relatively unconnected, But having high densities of green roofs would allow those patches to be more connected.

2:53 · Letting, for example, butterflies travel from patch to patch with lots of stepping stones of green roofs in between.

2:59 · And these are just three of the problems that green roofs can help solve.

conclusion

3:03 · But to really put a dent in any of these issues cities would have to implement green roofs at a large scale.

3:09 · Stuart Gaffin, the research scientist

you heard from earlier, estimates that if all of New York City’s 1 billion square feet of roof space were greened, those green roofs could lower the city’s

temperature by nearly two degrees Fahrenheit and absorb over 10 billion

gallons of stormwater each year.

3:27 · As cities continue to grow, all that empty roof space starts to look more and more like an opportunity, an untapped resource, a future ecosystem of problem-solving meadows in the sky.

How to Fix New York City’s $500M Trash Problem WSJ Pro Perfected

The Wall Street Journal, 08 14, 2026

Video (6:52)
Description

New York City handles nearly 3 million tons of trash a year. And much of it ends up on the curb in loose plastic bags — before getting shipped to landfills outside of the city altogether, on barges and then trains. This system costs the city around $500 million a year. So, can the largest city in the United States modernize its waste management?

WSJ explores how experts and city officials plan to tackle New York City’s giant trash problem.

Chapters:

0:00 NYC’s trash problem

0:34 Possible solutions

1:10 Why the issue exists

1:59 What the sanitation department does now

3:34 The bigger problem

6:13 The bigger solution

Pro Perfected

Experts in engineering and design break down a ubiquitous problem, examining how the world is built and what can make it better.

#NYC #Trash #WSJ

Transcript
NYC’s trash problem

0:00 · Every year, New York City handles nearly three million tons of trash. For decades, mountains of garbage bags have lined the streets, feeding rats and blocking sidewalks.

0:10 · Most people think that garbage goes up to solid waste heaven. It does quite the opposite.

0:15 · Now, the Sanitation Department says it has a solution. Its trash revolution program aims to finally put a lid on the countless garbage bags piling city blocks.

0:24 · But can redesigning the way New Yorkers throw out their trash solve a problem decades in the making?

0:29 · We asked people who know the system best. Steven Cohen is the Director of the Sustainability Management Program at Columbia University, and he says one of New York’s biggest solutions is surprisingly simple, changing how the city stores and collects its garbage. Instead of leaving flimsy trash bags on the sidewalk, the city’s sanitation department is now moving towards sealed containers emptied by mechanized trucks.

Possible solutions

0:55 · The motto of the Sanitation Department is New York’s strongest. And now I think we’re going to call them New York’s smartest because they figured out a way to get the machine to do the work.

1:04 · But changing how New York handles its garbage means undoing a system that’s been in place for more than half a century.

Why the issue exists

1:10 · We used to have metal cans that everybody had to put their garbage in that you would have to wash out after use. People in apartment buildings, superintendents didn’t like doing that so much.

1:21 · That’s how New Yorkers put out their trash until the late 1960s. But when sanitation workers went on strike in 1968, residents switched to plastic garbage bags. And when the strike ended, the bags stayed.

1:33 · This was really great news for the rats because it essentially created a buffet for them all over New York City of free food, because rats have no trouble at all getting into garbage bags.

1:43 · More than 50 years later, trash bags are just as common as the vermin they attract, and the city’s layout makes the problem even harder. Unlike other major American cities, Manhattan was built largely without alleys, leaving garbage with only one place to wait for pickup, the curb. The city is now trying to take those bags off the sidewalk altogether, replacing them with permanent containers like these.

What the sanitation department does now

2:05 · The model that’s used everywhere in the United States, either dumpsters in alleys, or 96 gallon sort of wheeled containers, wasn’t feasible in New York City, because of our density and because of the way that our city’s been built. So we looked to the model used in Europe, the stationary on-street containers, as the solution for a large number of our buildings.

2:27 · Buildings can store trash in these bins throughout the week, and they all get emptied on pickup days.

2:32 · You have a bin that’s made of plastic, that’s very durable, and there’s a lot of garbage in that bin. And you have a mechanical arm picking up a tremendous amount of garbage and dumping it into the truck. The worker you see here is operating a machine and he doesn’t have to go out and pick up the garbage himself.

2:52 · And these bins seem to be working to keep out pests.

2:55 · The pace is a little bit slower, but I think overall, I think for the community it’s great. It does, we’ve seen the difference. I know they’ve been trying to tackle the rat situation, and it’s definitely improved.

3:06 · Businesses have been required to use closed bins since March 2024. And next year, the city plans to expand these residential containers to six more districts. By 2031, officials say New York City will be fully containerized.

3:19 · This is a way to collect the garbage in a more efficient and frankly humane way, protecting the occupational health and safety of the workers. And I think this is really how robotics are changing all sorts of manual labor.

The bigger problem

3:34 · In Cohen’s vision for waste in the city, robotics would also play a larger role, sorting through the trash for recyclables to divert waste from landfills.

3:42 · Humans do not want to spend their day sorting garbage. Robots could care less. And what AI can do is train those robots to really be able to identify what’s coming past them. And as we get better at that, that waste stream suddenly becomes a trove of resources.

4:02 · That could address a much larger challenge for New York. The city has nowhere to put most of its garbage.

4:08 · The first place the waste goes in New York City is to a waste transfer station.

4:12 · New York once relied on its own landfills, but as they filled up, the city gradually closed them. Fresh Kills, which was the largest landfill in the world at the time, closed in 2001.

4:22 · We just ran out of space.

4:24 · Today, New York’s trash is offloaded at transfer stations, packed into shipping containers, and sent to landfills and waste-to-energy plants across the country. That process costs around $500 million per year. Waste-to-energy plants burn garbage to generate usable energy, offering an alternative to burying trash in landfills. But either way, the city still has to send its waste away.

4:45 · In the map here, what you see is that, once we could no longer landfill in the five boroughs, we had to go outside the city. And you can see we go already 1,000 miles or longer to find a place to bring our garbage. We go wherever we can.

5:05 · We’re constantly looking.

5:07 · That problem is only going to get worse.

5:10 · We are going to be forced to ship our trash further and further, to places in the south and the Midwest where there’s more space, there’s more landfills, there’s more capacity. And that’s going to become really expensive.

5:22 · Cohen’s proposal could change that equation by reducing how much waste needs to be shipped out of the city in the first place.

5:27 · The long-term vision I would have would be to build a waste processing plant that uses artificial intelligence and robotics to separate the useful resources and create separate places for those substances to be reused.

5:46 · This concept already exists.

5:48 · Materials processing facilities use automated systems to sort recyclables, but Cohen’s proposal is more ambitious, applying that technology to New York’s entire waste stream. Some tech companies are already experimenting with robots that can sort waste autonomously, separating out materials of all categories to be repurposed. In New York, Cohen says the technology would be economically transformative, using trash as a product to be sold rather than disposed of.

The bigger solution

6:13 · The question is how a city as large as New York can implement this technology in the long term. The Sanitation Department has multiple programs in place for recycling and composting, but the technology is still developing. So as New York’s trash disposal gets more expensive, recovering what’s inside could become increasingly valuable. But making that vision a reality requires a lot more than just rethinking the city’s trash collection.

6:36 · Anything that isn’t grown, anything that isn’t organic is finite. And at a certain point, digging it out of the planet becomes more expensive than digging it out of your garbage.

Superblocks How Barcelona is taking city streets back from cars

Vox, 09 27, 2016

Video (5:30)
Description

Modern cities are designed for cars. But the city of Barcelona is testing out an urban design trick that can give cities back to pedestrians.

Help us make more ambitious videos by joining the Vox Video Lab. It gets you exclusive perks, like livestream Q&As with all the Vox creators, a badge that levels up over time, and video extras bringing you closer to our work! Learn more at http://bit.ly/video-lab

Read more: http://www.vox.com/2016/8/4/12342806/barcelona-superblocks

Thumbnail image from http://shutterstock.com

Subscribe to our channel! http://goo.gl/0bsAjO

Vox.com is a news website that helps you cut through the noise and understand what’s really driving the events in the headlines. Check out http://www.vox.com to get up to speed on everything from Kurdistan to the Kim Kardashian app.

Check out our full video catalog: http://goo.gl/IZONyE

Follow Vox on Twitter: http://goo.gl/XFrZ5H

Or on Facebook: http://goo.gl/U2g06o

Transcript

0:01 · If you imagine a typical American city street, and you take away the space that’s dedicated to cars, you aren’t left with very much.

0:09 · There are some narrow walkways on the side, and some bridges in between them, but not much else.

0:13 · Cars dominate cities.

0:16 · Spend some time walking around most cities and you’ll find yourself pushed to narrow sidewalks, waiting for crosswalk lights.

0:20 · You’ll find cyclists navigating really narrow strips of space.

0:23 · Americans are used to cars the way that fish are used to water.

0:28 · That’s so ubiquitous in the U.S. that I think for most people, it just never occurred to them that it could be otherwise.

0:35 · But what if there were a way to change that?

0:36 · To give space back to pedestrians and bicyclists, and to make cities more friendly to life outside of a car?

0:43 · It turns out Barcelona might have a solution.

0:48 · In 2014, the city was faced with serious air pollution problems.

0:51 · Barcelona and its 35 surrounding municipalities consistently failed to meet the EU’s air quality targets.

0:57 · Studies were showing that air pollution in the region causes 3,500 premature deaths every year.

1:03 · Traffic in the city also causes severe noise pollution.

1:05 · So the city developed an extensive Urban Mobility Plan with the hope of reducing traffic by 21 percent.

1:11 · The coolest part of the plan were these things: They call them “superilles”.

1:17 · Superilles?

1:18 · “Si, superilles.”

1:19 · That translates to “superblocks”.

1:21 · It’s this urban design concept intended to minimize the presence of cars in city centers.

1:26 · The word “superblock” has been used before to describe huge city blocks without any passageways for cars.

1:31 · But that’s not what’s happening here.

1:33 · So here’s how Barcelona’s plan works.

1:35 · You take nine square city blocks and close off the inside to through traffic.

1:39 · So buses, big freight trucks — or any vehicles that are trying to get from one part of town to the next — have to drive around the perimeter.

1:47 · Inside the superblock, the speed limit is kept to 10 kilometers per hour — that translates to just over 6 miles per hour — and curbside parking is replaced by underground parking.

1:55 · That means you wind up with street space for markets, outdoor games, and events.

2:00 · Within this nine square block perimeter you’re gonna have kind of a pleasant streetscape where people can walk around and mingle and do things without this kind of constant fear of cars around.

2:13 · The concept is going to be tested out in five neighborhoods, but the city has identified 120 possible intersections throughout the region where it could be implemented.

2:22 · So how do we know what the results of this kind of plan would look like?

2:24 · Well, northwest of Barcelona is a city called Vitoria-Gasteiz, which has implemented superblock designs since 2008.

2:30 · In the main superblock at the city center, pedestrian space increased from 45 percent of the total surface area to 74 percent.

2:37 · With so much less traffic, noise levels dropped from 66.5 dBA to 61 dBA.

2:43 · Most impressive of all, there was a 42% reduction in nitrogen oxide emissions and a 38% reduction in particle pollution in the area.

2:51 · On top of that, business is up.

2:52 · What you consistently see when people change their streetscapes to prioritize human beings over cars is you don’t see any decline in economic activity, you see the opposite.

3:03 · You get more people walking and cycling around more slowly, stopping more often patronizing businesses more, and that … center of social activity will tend to build on itself.

3:15 · So here’s the question: could something like this work in an American city?

3:20 · Barcelona has some unique advantages getting started on this plan, in that a lot of it was built before cars, and a lot of it was built on a simple grid.

3:32 · The district of Eixample — where the superblock plan is based — was designed in 1859 in this repetitive grid structure by this guy, Ildefons Cerdá.

3:41 · He basically invented the word for (and the study of) “urbanization” when he laid out this grid plan for Barcelona that evenly distributed resources like schools and hospitals.

3:51 · But superblock designers insist that cities don’t need a simple grid structure to implement this kind of plan.

3:56 · It can work anywhere.

3:58 · Now, cities in the US have have attempted some car-minimizing projects like this.

4:01 · The problem is, they’re usually done in wealthier areas with lots of existing businesses.

4:07 · Zoning policies often require separation of residential and commercial areas — but an ideal walkable area would be a mix of the two.

4:14 · On top of that, zoning minimums on parking availability encourage the presence of cars and parking lots, and minimums on street width make for wide, unwalkable streets.

4:24 · Because of that, walkable districts are basically isolated luxury items in the US.

4:29 · What makes the Barcelona plan different is that they aren’t setting aside one fancy neighborhood or town square to make pedestrian-friendly — instead, by proposing superblocks throughout the entire city, they’ve declared car-free spaces a right for everybody, no matter what part of town they’re in.

4:44 · Maybe — this might be overly optimistic — but I think it has sunk in in the U. S. that the model whereby every city resident comes with a car — and drives a car everywhere – is just inherently limited.

4:57 · It limits the growth of your city, it limits the health of your city and the growth of your city.

5:01 · So one way or another we have to find ways of having a lot of people live close to one another without all of them having cars.

5:11 · You know, being able to get around and work and play in live and have enjoyable lives without cars.

Why did we build high-rise public housing projects

City Beautiful, 10 17, 2017

Video (6:48)
Description

Watch this video ad-free on Nebula: https://nebula.tv/videos/city-beautiful-why-did-we-build-high-rise-public-housing-projects

In the spring of 2007, the Chicago Housing Authority demolished the last of the 28 buildings in the Robert Taylor Homes housing project. Robert Taylor Homes is one of several high profile housing project demolitions, from St. Louis’s Pruitt-Igoe to fellow Chicago project Cabrini Green. The demolitions serve as an admission that high-rise public housing wasn’t a good idea. The housing that replaced them are all low rise projects better integrated within existing neighborhoods. So why did anyone back then think it was a good idea? Why did housing authorities build these high rise projects in the first place?

Resources on this topic:

Hunt, D. B. (2001). What Went Wrong with Public Housing in Chicago? A History of the Robert Taylor Homes. Journal of the Illinois State Historical Society, 94(1), 96–123.

“Demolished: The End of Chicago’s Public Housing” by David Eads and Helga Salinas. https://apps.npr.org/lookatthis/posts/publichousing/

“When Public Housing Was Paradise” by Joy Connelly https://openingthewindow.com/2011/06/13/when-public-housing-was-paradise-2/

“Elizabeth Wood, 93, Innovator In Early Days of Public Housing” by Bruce Lambert http://www.nytimes.com/1993/01/17/us/elizabeth-wood-93-innovator-in-early-days-of-public-housing.html

“A Brief History of Public Housing” by Jennifer Amy Stoloff https://www.researchgate.net/profile/Jennifer_Stoloff/publication/228789405_A_brief_history_of_public_housing/links/5643a68008ae9f9c13e05df6/A-brief-history-of-public-housing.pdf

Video sources: - Archive.org, Prelinger Archives

Photo sources: - Wikimedia Commons - Flickr user Samuel A. Love - Zahiri, N., Dezhdar, O., & Foroutan, M. (2016). Rethinking of Critical Regionalism in High-Rise Buildings. Buildings, 7(1), 4. https://doi.org/10.3390/buildings7010004

Filmed in sunny Sacramento, California.

We just asked this kind of preliminary question, “Do you know what air pollution is?”

Then we asked the second question, which was, “Does Houston have air pollution?”

And the significant number of children went from, “Yes.

Yeah, I understand what air pollution is,” to “No, Houston doesn’t have air pollution,” which completely blew us away.

How could these kids growing up in the most polluted city in the United States, how could they not know about that their city was polluted?

And that’s the start, for me, some 30 years ago, in thinking about this issue, about environmental generational amnesia.

That the children are coming of age, and they’re constructing a baseline of what normal is.

Shane, voice-over: That mouthful of a phrase, “environmental generational amnesia,” refers to the way that each generation grows up thinking that their experience of the world is normal.

So in our urbanizing world, the less nature we experience, the less we think we need.

With each generation, nature becomes smaller.

Wilderness becomes a city park and then a row of trees in a median and then…nothing at all.

Pearl: We are challenging ourselves as architects whether we can design better.

Shane, voice-over: Pearl is the architect who designed the Kampung Admiralty, the building we’re standing in now.

Pearl: Our design concept, it’s a very unique combination.

We actually have elderly housing, public housing, combined with healthcare, which is a medical center, commercial and some social programs, and a food court, yeah.

Shane: Oh, wow.

So that’s why it’s not gated.

It’s very open.

Anybody can actually come in and use the facilities here.

Shane, voice-over: Singapore is the third most densely populated city in the world.

The entire country is an island with only 281 square miles of land, but 5.5 million people live here.

In a city this dense, you wouldn’t expect much room for nature.

But it’s like they say: where there’s a will, there’s a way.

And by will, I mean law.

Legislation enables it to happen.

The building took the land.

So we need to do replacement.

The legislation actually requires a building to give back 100% landscape.

Oh!

100% of the site footprint has to be replaced into the building.

Every square inch of, like, green space that you take up, you have to put back in…

Put back in, yeah.

into the building somehow.

Into the building.

Shane, voice-over: A few years after completing the building, Pearl’s firm surveyed the animal life in Kampung Admiralty’s gardens.

50 different species had moved in.

Pearl: The species in this building is higher than the species on the ground park.

Oh, really?

Yeah, which is right nearby.

So…

Wait, so there’s more species within this construction than in, like, a natural space of the same amount of greenery?

Yeah, correct.

That’s incredible.

Thinking about an entire city built like this, basically, you’re talking about reforestation and urbanization as sort of one and the same.

Correct.

[Crows] Shane, voice-over: This development is a microcosm of what’s happening all over Singapore.

It’s not just one building or designer or architecture firm.

The whole city is teeming with life.

Narrator: It’s hard to believe that not so long ago, war surged around it and even in it.

Singapore, cosmopolis of the East.

Malays, Chinese, Indians, and British have taken time by the forelock, and the shops and market stalls dazzle the eye and make our mouths water.

As you sort of, like, move around the city now, you know, do you, you know, like, do you go to places and you’re like, “Oh, this is my baby”?

Yeah, yeah.

All the time.

[Laughter] Shane, voice-over: Professor Cheong Koon Hean is a music lover and life-long Singaporean.

She’s also a legendary urban planner.

A lot of folks credit her with shaping Singapore’s singular urban landscape.

BAER: Raise a toast to the GOAT, yeah Cheong: This is one of my favorite neighborhood centers in a public housing estate.

Because in Singapore, 80% of the people live in public housing in estates like this.

Oh, wow.

In highly affordable housing.

Shane, voice-over: Kampung Admiralty, the building Pearl Chee designed, is public housing, too.

And these places are cush.

The apartments are modern and clean, the amenities are incredible, and most developments are a 10-minute or less walk from public transportation.

And, of course, everything is draped in greenery, from the street level to the tops of the skyscrapers.

Where I come from, when we say “public housing,” basically, like, we call it “the projects.”

And I was actually raised a lot in public housing.

And that public housing and this public housing are not the same thing.

Well, you can’t believe it when you come to Singapore today, but, say, about 60, 70 years ago, actually, Singapore was a nation of slums and squatters.

It was also polluted and lots of traffic congestion.

Shane, voice-over: When Singapore gained its sovereignty from Britain in 1959, it was a city of 1.5 million people and massive, unplanned sprawl.

Tens of thousands of people had no place to live.

So the newly elected government created the HDB, or Housing and Development Board.

Cheong: And within 5 years, the HDB, they already built 51,000 units of flats.

It started from very basic housing, but now, of course, the quality is a lot better, as you can see around you.

Yeah.

Shane, voice-over: And public housing is just the beginning.

Singapore is a very small city-state.

It’s actually only half the size of metropolitan London.

Oh, wow.

But we are a country.

We’re not just a city.

So everything you need about a country, whether it’s for water, power generation, ports, airports, plus housing, shops, offices, industry, all has to be built within this tiny island city-state.

And so biodiversity and ecological balance became really quite an important concept.

Now, as we developed the land, of course we start to lose space for planting.

So what did we do?

We decided to move the greenery upwards into the sky.

And so, what you did is you just created new space.

And suddenly, you bring back all the biodiversity.

Back in the States, urban areas and wildlife are sort of like oil and water.

They don’t mix very well.

But what you’ve done here is something very, very different.

Over the years, we moved from being a garden city to a city in the garden because we started to plant so extensively.

And in the last decade or so, we moved from a city in a garden to city in nature.

In a place where you have limited space and you have this growing population that there are two very sort of competing needs.

Shane, absolutely, there’s always competing needs, right?

So it’s really this very important balancing act of allocating the right amount of land for different uses to create this type of an environment.

Shane, voice-over: And who orchestrates this balancing act?

It’s the Singaporean government, with their comprehensive “Master Plan” for managing the city-state’s ongoing growth.

By centralizing the control of urban development, Singapore paves the way– you see what I did there– for projects that fuse function and form.

Cheong: If you look behind us, the Punggol Waterway is actually a man-made river.

We dammed up two rivers to become freshwater reservoirs.

Now, the engineers wanted to connect the two reservoirs with a pipe– very functional.

But then the architects and the planners say, “Why do a pipe?

Why don’t we make a river?”

And so, this river connected the two reservoirs to balance and optimize the storage of water, but at the same time, it became a leisure facility.

So Punggol has a nickname.

It’s the Venice of Singapore.

Oh, that’s amazing.

Because we brought in water, right?

It has its aesthetics, but everything also serves… Function, yes, a function.

a very specific function.

Even the Punggol Waterway, there’s a family of otters that have come back.

Oh, that’s incredible.

They live on the Punggol Waterway.

Shane, voice-over: Singapore challenges a lot of what I thought I knew about cities.

No one’s saying that we should all be like Singapore, or even that we could be.

This place is unique.

90% of Singapore’s land is publicly owned.

The government determines what gets built and where.

And with everyone sharing the same tiny island, by definition, they’re all in this together.

Most places don’t have all that.

But I’m not sure we need it to re-imagine what cities can be.

I think the thing that stands out to me about Singapore is the intentionality.

I’m not sure if this specific model can be applied anywhere or everywhere, but I think they’re doing things that I think other cities can learn from.

We have to get our act together to make cities effective.

Creativity, innovation, and that will to survive and to want to do well are very, very strong drivers.

That’s human beings, right?

That’s what we are.

Shane, voice-over: Cities might just be humankind’s most remarkable invention.

Because we thought we were building ourselves a place to live, but we were really creating a new ecosystem, a place where nature has to adapt to live alongside us and some of our urban neighbors are more welcome than others.

City living brings challenges– for us and all the species that share our urban spaces.

But we shouldn’t forget that cities can also help us flourish.

In Oakland, Chris said something that stuck with me.

We are a communal species.

So for folks to think, “Well, if you just get outside the city, you’ll be OK,” nah, nah, nah, it doesn’t work like that.

We need to be able to have connection.

Shane, voice-over: That’s why cities aren’t going anywhere and why we need to make them better.

With a little creativity and vision, they can deliver that connection– to each other and maybe to the rest of our incredible planet, too.
